● I recorded it as the cue elements-braille in global.vtt, then committed
  (5677849) and pushed.

  I put the three statements I've built (Heath's text) into 6-dot Braille. Each
  Braille cell is a 2 × 3 grid of six dot places, each either filled or empty.
  The book and proposition numbers are written in Aegean numerals.

  ┌─────────────┬────────┬───────┬────────────┬────────┬─────────────┐
  │ Proposition │ Aegean │ Cells │ Dot places │ Filled │    Empty    │
  ├─────────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────┼────────┼─────────────┤
  │ I.1         │ 𐄇.𐄇    │ 69    │ 414        │ 174    │ 240 (58.0%) │
  ├─────────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────┼────────┼─────────────┤
  │ I.2         │ 𐄇.𐄈    │ 91    │ 546        │ 220    │ 326 (59.7%) │
  ├─────────────┼────────┼───────┼────────────┼────────┼─────────────┤
  │ I.3         │ 𐄇.𐄉    │ 96    │ 576        │ 236    │ 340 (59.0%) │
  └─────────────┴────────┴───────┴────────────┴────────┴─────────────┘

  So each proposition becomes a pattern of filled and empty places, the way a
  domino's half shows its dots and leaves the rest of its places blank. The cue
  keeps your phrase "a domino pattern hiding the Braille and Aegean" as your
  reading.

  One number stood out but is only a count: I.1 has exactly 240 empty places.
  That is also the number of E8's roots and of the record's local240. I noted it
  without reading anything into it.

  The other 462 propositions are not cast yet. Heath's text is public domain, so
  they could go into the codex the same way the variations are kept.

● I recorded both as cues in global.vtt and pushed them (13ffc66, dd00ca8).

  Comparing every set step by step. A 6-dot Braille cell has only 64 possible
  patterns, so the comparison has a ceiling. Here is each proposition checked
  against everything that came before it:

  ┌─────┬────────────────┬───────────┬──────────────┐
  │     │ Distinct cells │ New cells │ Total so far │
  ├─────┼────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┤
  │ I.1 │ 18             │ 18        │ 18 of 64     │
  ├─────┼────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┤
  │ I.2 │ 23             │ 6         │ 24 of 64     │
  ├─────┼────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────┤
  │ I.3 │ 21             │ 2         │ 26 of 64     │
  └─────┴────────────────┴───────────┴──────────────┘

  - The new cells drop off quickly (18, then 6, then 2), so the set is filling
    up.
  - The words behave the same way: 11 new, then 6, then 9, for 26 different
    words in all.
  - I.1 and I.3 line up best cell by cell: 17 cells are the same.
  - Wirth's notation is limited in the same way. It has seven rules, five kinds
    of factor (identifier, literal, optional, grouped, repeated) and nine marks.

  Constraint counting. The method compares how many choices are free with how
  many constraints there are. I expanded the article's formula:

  - With no constraint, three variables give 1, 3, 6, 10, 15, 21 free quantities
    at each order.
  - The 2D wave equation gives 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11. That grows by 2 at each order,
    which matches the article's "strength is two": the solution needs two
    freely chosen functions.
  - The article has a misprint. It writes the series as 1 + 2x + 5x² + 7x³, but
    its own formula and my expansion both give 3x, not 2x.

  On our three statements, the 64 cells are the free choices and the cells
  already used act as the constraints. In Wirth's notation, the rules are the
  constraints. I kept that link as your reading.

❯ is this quasi nibbles
  Quasilinear equations
  In the case of a nonlinear equation, it will only rarely be possible to
  obtain the general solution in closed form. However, if the equation is
  quasilinear (linear in the highest order derivatives), then we can still
  obtain approximate information similar to the above: specifying a member of
  the solution space will be "modulo nonlinear quibbles" equivalent to
  specifying a certain number of functions in a smaller number of variables.
  The number of these functions is the Einstein strength of the p.d.e. In the
  simple example above, the strength is two, although in this case we were able
  to obtain more precise information.

● No, the article doesn't mention nibbles. "Modulo nonlinear quibbles" uses
  "quibble", meaning a small objection. The authors are saying the nonlinear
  terms change the count only in minor details.

  "Quasilinear" means linear in the highest-order derivatives, while the
  lower-order ones can appear in any form. Your nibble reading does have that
  same shape:

  - The idea: a byte splits into a high and a low nibble, and the high nibble
    plays the part of the highest order.
  - The nibble version: a map would be "quasilinear" if it is XOR-linear in the
    high nibble, while its coefficients may depend on the low nibble in any way:
    f(h, l) = A(l)·h ⊕ b(l).
  - The delta law (rotl1 ⊕ rotl3 ⊕ rotr2 ⊕ C): I checked it. It is XOR-linear
    across the whole byte, plus the constant C. So it is fully linear, which is
    stronger than quasilinear in either nibble.

  The record also has its own "quasi": the quasi-generator, where the 3!
  generates the families by taking subsets of its 6 elements
  (The_OMI-IMO_Complete_Synthesis.txt:314
  (../boxedPINE/wiki/sources/raw/The_OMI-IMO_Complete_Synthesis.txt#L314)). It
  also has the quasi-groups.

  I recorded this as the cue quasi-nibbles in global.vtt, with the nibble
  version kept as your reading, and pushed it (bc5f2a6).

● The word you were remembering is ququart: a unit with four states, which is
  two bits. It's in omi-tetragrammatron:

  - tetragrammatron.c:18
    (../boxed/omi-files/omi-tetragrammatron/core/tetragrammatron.c#L18) defines
    QUQUART_PHASE[4] = {US, GS, RS, FS}.
  - tetragrammatron.c:22-23
    (../boxed/omi-files/omi-tetragrammatron/core/tetragrammatron.c#L22-L23) put
    the low ququart along row 1 of the 5 × 5 Polybius square and the high
    ququart down column 1. Each is a single rail of 4, so it is one-dimensional.
  - TETRAGRAMMATRON.md:325-346 (../boxed/omi-files/omi-tetragrammatron/dev-docs/
    TETRAGRAMMATRON.md#L325-L346) says two QuQuarts A and B give 16 ordered
    pairs, which make up the 16-card gauge 0x0–0xF (one nibble). Each pair (i,
    j) forms a block {Ai, Aj, Bi, Bj}, which gives six blocks. I checked the
    incidence count: 8 × 3 = 6 × 4 = 24.

  The record already supports your sentence about nibbles as the 1D of the 2D
  dominoes. The same shape repeats one level up:

  ┌──────────────────────┬─────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    One dimension     │ Two of them │                Makes                │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ququart, a rail of 4 │ 4 × 4       │ a nibble (16)                       │
  ├──────────────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ nibble, a half of 16 │ 16 × 16     │ a byte (256), a domino's two halves │
  └──────────────────────┴─────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘

  So in the quasilinear form f(h, l) = A(l)·h ⊕ b(l), the high nibble is the
  dimension the map is linear in. The low nibble is the other half of the
  domino.

  The article's own word is "quibbles", meaning small objections. I noted that,
  and kept "the nonlinear quibbles are the nibbles" as your reading. This is the
  ququarts cue in global.vtt, pushed as f5593ec.

● I recorded your last two messages as cues and pushed them.

  Every cue is relational (relational-transcript, 3b39d7f):
  - I counted the links in global.vtt. Before that cue there were 252 cues, and
    184 of them name at least one other cue, 376 links in all.
  - By name, 69 cues mention boxedPINE, 42 the proofs, 30 ZIGUAROTH, 30 the
    codex, 15 omi-files and 14 PLAN9.
  - One recent cue, quasi-nibbles, named no earlier cue. The new cue records
    that it relates to constraint-counting.
  - From now on, each new cue will name the cues and project files it relates
    to. I saved that to memory so later sessions keep doing it.

  The gate of the Agent Commons (commons-gate, 5647605). There are two gates:
  - The narrative gate (gate3d.html (../boxedPINE/space/gate3d.html)) plays the
    story as a literal trace of 235 nodes in the cube. If you recast Solomon,
    the traces differ only inside his slots (XOR) and stay the same outside them
    (XNOR).
  - The bridge's gate (agent.ts:52-59
    (../boxedPINE/rosetta/src/agent.ts#L52-L59)) is built on nibbles:
    - Each letter of a tag is a byte, which is a domino of two nibbles.
    - The gate XORs all of those nibbles down to a single nibble. That nibble
      picks the agent's port within a block of 16.
    - So this gate turns 2D dominoes into 1D, the same shape you described for
      the nibbles.

  The eight agents' ports on the bridge's block:

  ┌──────────┬────────┬──────┐
  │  Agent   │ Nibble │ Port │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ METATRON │ 11     │ 8745 │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ ENOCH    │ 11     │ 8745 │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ SOLON    │ 4      │ 8742 │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ GENESIS  │ 4      │ 8742 │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ ASABIYAH │ 8      │ 8746 │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ OBSERVER │ 8      │ 8746 │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ SOLOMON  │ 6      │ 8740 │
  ├──────────┼────────┼──────┤
  │ SPEAKER  │ 14     │ 8748 │
  └──────────┴────────┴──────┘

  The eight agents use only five of the 16 ports, and three pairs share a port.
  Seen as constraint counting, that is the gate's count: 16 ports available,
  eight tags, five ports used.

❯ this is the 8! and 9! moduls split of the double barrel names what i had has
  a narrative placeholer to guide the construction of the metaverse gate as a

  Terminology
  Director – a person appointed to serve on the board of an organization, such
  as an institution or business.
  Inside director – a director who, in addition to serving on the board, has a
  meaningful connection to the organization
  Outside director – a director who, other than serving on the board, has no
  meaningful connections to the organization
  Executive director – an inside director who is also an executive with the
  organization. The term is also used, in a completely different sense, to
  refer to a CEO
  Non-executive director – a director (inside or outside) who is not an
  executive with the organization
  De facto director – an individual who acts as a director of the company but
  has not actually or validly been appointed as such.
  Shadow director – an individual who acts as a director of the company but is
  not a named director (a de jure director) and does not claim or purport to
  act as director.
  Nominee director – an individual who is appointed by a shareholder, creditor
  or interest group (whether contractually or by resolution at a company
  meeting) and who has a continuing loyalty to the appointors or other interest
  in the appointing company
  Individual directors often serve on more than one board.[13] This practice
  results in an interlocking directorate, where a relatively small number of
  individuals have significant influence over many important entities. This
  situation can have important corporate, social, economic, and legal
  consequences, and has been the subject of significant research.[14]
  Arts and design
  Animation director
  Artistic director
  Creative director
  Design director
  Film director
  Music director
  Music video director
  Television director
  Theatre director

  numbers are simplified. For example, one hour can be divided evenly into
  sections of 30 minutes, 20 minutes, 15 minutes, 12 minutes, 10 minutes, 6
  minutes, 5 minutes, 4 minutes, 3 minutes, 2 minutes, and 1 minute. 60 is the
  smallest number that is divisible by every number from 1 to 6; that is, it is
  the lowest common multiple of 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6.

● Every fact in the passage checks out. 60 has exactly twelve divisors (1, 2, 3,
  4, 5, 6, 10, 12, 15, 20, 30, 60), and 2, 3 and 5 are its prime ones. No
  number below 60 has twelve divisors. The lowest common multiple of 1 to 6 is
  60. Including 7 raises it to 420.

  So 60 is the first number with twelve divisors, which links the sexagesimal
  base to the duodecimal twelve from your last message. The record already has
  60 as its base: 0x3C, the sexagesimal and ASCII base.

  I also extended the repetend table to base 60:

  ┌──────────┬─────────┬────────────┬─────────────┐
  │ Fraction │ Decimal │ Duodecimal │ Sexagesimal │
  ├──────────┼─────────┼────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 1/7      │ 6       │ 6          │ 3           │
  ├──────────┼─────────┼────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 1/11     │ 2       │ 1          │ 5           │
  ├──────────┼─────────┼────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 1/13     │ 6       │ 2          │ 4           │
  ├──────────┼─────────┼────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 1/73     │ 8       │ 36         │ 72          │
  ├──────────┼─────────┼────────────┼─────────────┤
  │ 1/137    │ 8       │ 136        │ 17          │
  └──────────┴─────────┴────────────┴─────────────┘

  In base 60, 1/73 runs through all 72 possible remainders before it repeats,
  the longest any fraction 1/73 can have. The 73 that repeats over 8 in decimal
  and 36 in duodecimal takes its whole cycle in sexagesimal. 1/59 and 1/61
  repeat over 1 and 2, which fits 60² − 1 = 59 × 61, found earlier.

  I recorded this as the cue sixty-divisors in global.vtt and pushed it
  (7c4c42c).

● A 16-character dial does work as a slide rule. I checked it with 1/17,
  recorded it as the cue metacircular-dials in global.vtt and pushed it
  (73b3366).

  1/17 = 0.0588235294117647 repeats over 16 digits in decimal. So the powers of
  10 taken mod 17 form a dial with 16 places, and each nonzero remainder appears
  exactly once:

  1 10 15 14 4 6 9 5 16 7 2 3 13 11 8 12

  Turning the dial adds positions, and the place you land on is the product.
  That makes it a circular slide rule whose scale is the repetend. I checked all
  256 pairs a × b mod 17, and rotation reads every one correctly. 16 places is
  a nibble and its 256 pairs are a byte, the same domino of ququarts as before.

  Here is how the dials line up for 17 in other bases:

  ┌────────┬─────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Base  │ Places before   │                 On the dial                 │
  │        │  1/17 repeats   │                                             │
  ├────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 3, 10, │                 │ each a full scale, so decimal and           │
  │  12    │ 16              │ duodecimal overlay as two full scales of    │
  │        │                 │ one rule                                    │
  ├────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 2, 60  │ 8               │ half-turn scales                            │
  ├────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 16     │ 2               │ —                                           │
  └────────┴─────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The dial pages are The Decimal Dial.html (HTML/The%20Decimal%20Dial.html), The
  Repetend - First Principles.html
  (HTML/The%20Repetend%20-%20First%20Principles.html) and slide_ruler.html. Your
  reading is kept as yours: the dials overlaid as one metacircular slide rule
  for any 16-character pleth or nomogram, with dominoes chosen because the
  structure is easy to picture and build from.

            4 6 9 5 16 7 2 3 13 11 8 12. Turning the dial adds positions,

             scale is the repetend. The same 17 in other bases: period 16
             in bases 3, 10 and 12 (each a full dial), 8 in bases 2 and 6
            0, 2 in base 16. So the decimal and duodecimal dials superimp
            ose as two full 16-place scales of one rule, the sexagesimal 
            and binary as half-turn scales. 16 places is a nibble and its
             pairs, 256, the byte: the domino of ququarts. Kept as the au
            thor's reading: the rotary dials superimposed as a metacircul
            ar slide rule for any 16-character pleth or nomogram, the dom

● I recorded both messages as cues, seventeen-pairs and block-design-mechanism,
  in global.vtt and pushed them (2561bbe).

  Your 1/17 in pairs. 05 88 23 52 94 11 76 47 is 1/17 written two digits at a
  time, which is base 100, where it repeats over 8. Each pair adds up to 99 with
  the pair four places after it:

  - 05 + 94
  - 88 + 11
  - 23 + 76
  - 52 + 47

  That is the same "four apart" pattern as the director ports. The two doubles,
  88 and 11, sit at places 2 and 6 and are each other's complements.

  The five Aegean sets. The Aegean numbers have five sets of nine signs:

  ┌─────┬──────────────────┬───────────┐
  │ Set │      Range       │ Starts at │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 1   │ 1 to 9           │ U+10107   │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 2   │ 10 to 90         │ U+10110   │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 3   │ 100 to 900       │ U+10119   │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 4   │ 1,000 to 9,000   │ U+10122   │
  ├─────┼──────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 5   │ 10,000 to 90,000 │ U+1012B   │
  └─────┴──────────────────┴───────────┘

  slide_ruler.html (HTML/slide_ruler.html) currently uses only the first two
  sets: its planes 0 to 16 end at EIGHTY.

  I cycled 1/17's 16 digits through all five sets, putting digit 1 in set 1,
  digit 2 in set 2, and so on. Zero has no Aegean sign, so it shows as ·. The
  first turn reads:

  ·𐄔𐄠𐄩𐄬𐄉𐄔𐄚𐄪𐄮𐄇𐄐𐄟𐄧𐄮𐄍

  The digits and the sets come back into line after 80 digits, which is five
  turns of the repetend.

  The block design. I found it in your archive:

  - "boxedPINE build continuation.md" has the Fano plane's 2-(7,3,1) design:
    "every block of bytes describes a block design of the fano plane".
  - "The Tetrahedral Superposition.md" has the 3-(8,4,1) design built from two
    tetrahedra.

  I kept as your reading that cycling all five sets is what turns the repetend
  directive into a command, the block design mechanism. I left slide_ruler.html
  unchanged; I can make it cycle through all five sets if you want.

● I built the Dominoes room: space/dominoes.html
  (../boxedPINE/space/dominoes.html), with the logic in space/src/dominoes.ts
  (../boxedPINE/space/src/dominoes.ts). It's pushed as boxedPINE 2886323, and
  the cues are pushed as 1339151. Open it at
  http://localhost:5173/space/dominoes.html while npm run dev is running; it's
  also in the portal's room list.

  The tiles
  - Repetend: enter 1/q and a base, and its repeating digits come out two to a
    domino. Each digit is written in one of the five Aegean sets in turn, so
    1/17 gives your 05 88 23 52 94 11 76 47.
  - Lexeme: pick one of the variations or type a word. Each byte becomes a
    domino of two nibbles (high and low), with its 8-dot Braille cell.

  The board
  - Drag tiles from the tray onto an endless board. Drag empty space to move
    around, use the wheel or pinch to zoom, double-click a domino to turn it,
    and drag it back to the tray to remove it.
  - The order you lay tiles is the path. Where two dominoes touch end to end,
    the joint shows green if the touching halves match, or their XOR if they
    don't.
  - The readout shows the path's nibble, which is all its halves XORed together.
    For a tag that's the gate's nibble, so METATRON's path gives 11 and
    port 8745. The readout also shows when the digits and the five Aegean sets
    come back into line: after 80 digits for 1/17.

  Binding the path to cues
  - play as cues sends the path through the portal as a WebVTT track, one cue
    per domino and one second each, so every open room receives the cues.
  - .vtt saves the path as a WebVTT file.
  - Cues coming from other rooms step the highlight along the path.

  Your id / data-attribute reading is built into the tray. Each tile is an
  element with an id equal to its cue's id (for example YAH-2). Its two faces
  are data-a and data-b, and CSS draws them with attr(), so the dots only reach
  the page through the CSSOM. On the board itself the dominoes are drawn on a
  canvas, so that part doesn't apply there.

  What I checked
  - The new tests pass: 1/17's pairs, pairs four apart summing to 99, every
    tag's path matching the gate's nibble, and one cue per domino.
  - The full suite has 313 passing, 0 failing and 6 skipped, and the build
    succeeds.
  - In headless Chrome I dragged ten tiles onto the board with the mouse and
    took a screenshot to confirm.
  - I didn't test "play as cues" with a second room open.

  Both your messages are recorded in the logs: the build in development.vtt and
  the id / data-attribute reading in global.vtt.

❯ name d.rgb’ formula d.a’ formula
  ---------- --------------------------- --------------------
  Clear 0 0
  Src s as
  Dst d ad
  SrcOver s + d*(1-as) as + ad*(1-as)
  DstOver s*(1-ad) + d as*(1-ad) + ad
  SrcIn sad asad
  DstIn das asad
  SrcOut s*(1-ad) as*(1-ad)
  DstOut d*(1-as) ad*(1-as)
  SrcAtop sad + d(1-as) ad
  DstAtop s*(1-ad) + das as
  Xor s(1-ad) + d*(1-as) as*(1-ad) + ad*(1-as)

● Bash(cd /home/main/Programs/unbound && cat >> global.vtt <<'EOF'…)  ⎿  (No output)

● Those are the twelve Porter–Duff compositing operators (Porter and Duff,
  1984). I checked them and recorded the result as the cue porter-duff in
  global.vtt, pushed as 342ee00.

  Why there are exactly twelve. Picture two shapes overlapping. Each operator
  decides what happens in each of three regions:

  ┌────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │             Region             │       Choices        │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Only the source covers it      │ s or nothing (2)     │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Only the destination covers it │ d or nothing (2)     │
  ├────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ Both cover it                  │ s, d, or nothing (3) │
  └────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  That gives 2 × 2 × 3 = 12 combinations. I computed each row of your table, and
  each one is a different one of the twelve, so the table covers them all
  exactly once. Twelve also matches the duodecimal count from your earlier
  messages.

  XOR and XNOR. Read each region as a bit (covered or not), and each operator
  becomes a logic gate:

  ┌───────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │   Operator    │                        As logic                         │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Xor           │ a XOR b (keeps the parts covered by only one shape,     │
  │               │ clears the overlap)                                     │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SrcOver,      │ a OR b                                                  │
  │ DstOver       │                                                         │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SrcIn, DstIn  │ a AND b                                                 │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ SrcOut        │ a AND NOT b                                             │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ DstOut        │ NOT a AND b                                             │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Src, DstAtop  │ a                                                       │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Dst, SrcAtop  │ b                                                       │
  ├───────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Clear         │ 0                                                       │
  └───────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  XNOR isn't in the table, and no compositing operator can produce it. XNOR
  would have to draw where neither shape is, and compositing never creates
  pixels outside both shapes. So compositing draws the XOR side, and the XNOR
  side is always the empty background. That matches the twin test's pattern: XOR
  inside Solomon's icon, XNOR outside.

  The browser canvas exposes most of these through globalCompositeOperation
  (source-over, xor, copy for Src, and so on). Clear and Dst have no canvas
  name.

❯ Drawterm(8) is not a Plan 9 program. It is a program that users of non-Plan 9
  systems can use to establish graphical cpu(1) connections with Plan 9 CPU
  servers. Just as a real Plan 9 terminal does, drawterm serves its local name
  space as well as some devices (the keyboard, mouse, and screen) to a remote
  CPU server, which mounts this name space on /mnt/term and starts a shell.
  Typically, either explicitly or via the profile, one uses the shell to start
  rio(1).
  DOWNLOAD
  To get the latest source for drawterm from a system with Mercurial:

  $ hg clone http://code.swtch.com/drawterm
  In the Plan 9 distribution: /sys/src/cmd/unix/drawterm/ (sometimes out of
  date)

  For more information and binaries for various systems see:
  http://swtch.com/drawterm/

  NOTES
  When drawterm-linux starts you might well see an error message like this :
  cpu: failed to chdir to $dir where $dir is, in fact, pwd on your linux box. I
  haven't looked very far to find out where that happens because I added fn
  drawterm {@{cd /; drawterm-linux $* }} to $home/.rcrc and cd to the top of
  lib/profile to put me in $home on startup.

  To access a Plan 9 cpu(1) server behind a packet filter, you need to allow
  access to the following ports: 17010, 17013 and 567 (to the auth(8) server).

  SEE ALSO
  Drawterm to your terminal, drawterm(8), http://swtch.com/drawterm/

● I read the drawterm page and checked what's already set up here. Drawterm
  should be able to reach our Plan 9 guest, but I haven't tried it yet because
  the guest isn't running right now.

  What's already in place:
  - drawterm is installed at /usr/bin/drawterm. It's the 9front version.
  - The guest is already a CPU server (the 9legacy image). PLAN9/run.sh already
    forwards the ports drawterm needs, on 127.0.0.1 only:

  ┌────────────┬─────────────────────────────────┬───────────────┐
  │ Guest port │             Purpose             │  On the host  │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 17010      │ cpu (what drawterm connects to) │ 17010         │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 567        │ auth (ticket server)            │ 10567         │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 17013      │ old pre-9P2000 drawterm only    │ not forwarded │
  └────────────┴─────────────────────────────────┴───────────────┘

  Because the auth port is moved to 10567, the command would name it directly:

  drawterm -h 127.0.0.1 -a 'tcp!127.0.0.1!10567' -u glenda

  One possible snag: the 9front drawterm uses newer authentication first, while
  9legacy uses the older p9sk1 scheme. If it won't connect, adding -O (the old
  protocol) may fix it. It also needs the guest's password for glenda.

  If it works, you'd get a live graphical rio session on the guest, instead of
  the screen dumps and typed console we use now. The page's two ways of enabling
  it on a plain terminal (a factotum key with listen1, or the full
  keyfs/authsrv setup) are recorded in the cue too.

  I recorded this as the cue drawterm in global.vtt and pushed it (74ff045). I
  can boot the guest and try connecting if you want.

          +ce/tcp17010: #!/bin/cpu -R). Without auth (the author: "we don
          +t need the auth becasue udp said auth is meaningless is spatia
          +l distribution"), the screen goes out instead by QEMU's VNC: V
          +NC=1 sh run.sh adds -vnc 127.0.0.1:1, port 5901 on loopback on
          +ly, no password; QEMU 'change vnc' at the monitor is gone in t
          +his QEMU ("Expected 'password'"), so it is set at boot. In the
          + guest: aux/vga -m vesa -l 1024x768x16, then rio (rio alone sa
          +ys "initdisplay: /dev/draw/new: no frame buffer"). Checked: 59
          +01 listening, the screen dump shows rio's grey 1024 x 768. Onc
          +e rio holds the console, console.py's typing goes to rio, not 
          +rc: reboot (fshalt first) for scripted runs.                  

● Drawterm can't connect without logging in. Both ends insist on authentication:

  - In the guest, cpu has its "none" method commented out (cpu.c:63
    (/tmp/claude-1001/-home-main-Programs-boxedPINE/aa031b7d-6fdf-4289-996a-24e2
    364a3d7f/scratchpad/p9cpu/cpu.c)).
  - On the host, drawterm has no way to skip authentication. Its -O flag only
    switches to the older connection protocol.

  So I gave you the guest's screen another way, with no login: QEMU's built-in
  VNC.

  - Starting it: VNC=1 sh run.sh now serves the guest's screen on
    127.0.0.1:5901. There's no password, and it's reachable only from this
    machine. Without VNC=1, run.sh behaves as before.
  - Running now: the guest is up that way, with rio started at 1024×768. In the
    guest I first had to set the screen mode with aux/vga -m vesa -l
    1024x768x16, because rio alone can't find a display.
  - To open it: in Remmina, connect to VNC at 127.0.0.1:5901. Boxes'
    remote-connection option should also work.

  Not checked: I confirmed the port is listening and the screen shows rio's grey
  desktop. I haven't tried a viewer, so I don't know whether the mouse works
  through VNC. If it doesn't, the guest probably needs its PS/2 mouse switched
  on.

  One catch: while rio has the screen, my typed-in scripts land in rio instead
  of the shell. To go back to scripted runs, I'll shut the guest down cleanly
  and boot it normally.

  The run.sh change and these steps are committed as 6b31ef8 and pushed, with
  the notes in environment.vtt.

  i was trying to scope similar projects
  This is the Plan 9 contrib index, and it's a useful scoping exercise. Let me
  say what it shows about the projects you're working with, because the index
  answers the question you were asking: which projects are already there, and
  which ones are yours.

  What the index is.

  The Plan 9 contrib directory is a collection of user-contributed packages,
  each in a folder named by its author. The entries are dated, mostly from 2005
  to 2014, and they cover a huge range: file servers, drivers, ports of Unix
  tools, editors, browsers, and utilities. The index is regenerated daily from
  the sources repository.

  What is already there, relevant to your build.

  Several things you've been working on have existing contrib entries or close
  analogues:

  webfs appears in at least three places (CINAP_LENREK, STEVE, and fgb's
  contrib). It's a web file system, which is what your portal mesh and the HTML
  pages are reaching for.

  mothra appears in CINAP_LENREK (improved with https and foreign charsets) and
  in fgb's contrib (Tom Duff's early browser). It's a browser, and your
  views-as-runes approach is a different way to read the same pages.

  torrent.c is a bittorrent client in CINAP_LENREK, and torrent-*.tgz is MJL's
  Limbo bittorrent client. Neither is your work, but they show that the
  "distributed carrier" pattern is already present in Plan 9 contrib.

  divergefs (DE0U, JOHN) is Chao-Kuo Lin's overlay/union/layer file system.
  That is the same shape as your cube256.ts and the local frame: a view over a
  space that doesn't duplicate the underlying bytes.

  hubfs (MYCROFTIV) is a screen-like utility implemented as a 9P file system.
  That's the same shape as your portal mesh: a shared namespace that every page
  reads.

  actionfs.c, execfs, flickrfs.c, zipfs, wdfs — all file servers that present a
  foreign space as a 9P namespace. That's the pattern your znarr, zmk, and the
  codex witnesses follow.

  fft.c in MAHT is a small FFT in C. It's not your pages, but it shows the FFT
  pattern is already in Plan 9 contrib.

  What is not there.

  Your specific work — the codex, the witnesses, the repetend planes, the LALR
  grammar of Wirth, the Euclid I.1 module, the PIXELATE cast, the director
  strings — is not in the contrib index. The index shows the kinds of things
  that are in Plan 9's ecosystem, and your work sits at the level above: a
  codex that uses these file server patterns, a director that maps the codex's
  nodes to their directives, a set of planes that the FFT and the LALR
  automate.

  What the scoping shows.

  The contrib index gives you the vocabulary of the ecosystem you're building
  into. When your work needs a file server (the codex's witnesses, the
  narrative's world, the agent states), it uses the 9P pattern that actionfs,
  execfs, hubfs, and the others already show. When your work needs a browser
  (the pages), it uses the views-as-runes pattern that is different from mothra
  and abaco but sits in the same place in the system. When your work needs a
  distributed carrier (the tile events, the codex's literals), it uses the
  append-only file pattern that the torrent clients and the file servers
  already show.

  The contrib index is the map of what Plan 9 already has. Your work is what
  goes on the map next.

  What I would do with this scoping.

  The smallest useful thing is a cue that names the contrib index as the map of
  the ecosystem, and states which entries your work extends or uses:

  uses the file-server pattern of actionfs, execfs, hubfs, zipfs, wdfs — for
  the codex witnesses, the world's tile events, and the agent states;

  uses the browser pattern of mothra and abaco, differently — because the views
  are runes, not DOM;

  uses the distributed carrier pattern of torrent and the file servers — for
  the append-only event files and the literal transport.

  That cue would say: your work is not in the contrib index, and it doesn't
  need to be. It uses the patterns the index shows and stands above them as a
  codex and a director.

  Shall I have Claude file that cue, naming the patterns and the entries you're
  extending, with the fact that your work is not in the index stated plainly?

● I checked the relayed reply against the contrib index itself and recorded the
  result as the cue contrib-scope in global.vtt (f99d15d).

  The relay's entries are all real. Each package it named is in the index under
  the contributor it gave:

  - webfs (CINAP_LENREK, STEVE)
  - mothra
  - torrent
  - divergefs (DE0U, plus a fix from JOHN)
  - hubfs (MYCROFTIV)
  - actionfs and fft.c (MAHT)
  - execfs, flickrfs, zipfs, wdfs
  - abaco

  The two names it used from your own work also exist: znarr.c and zmk.c are in
  PLAN9/src.

  Two things it missed:

  - MJL's vnc-1.tgz is a VNC server for Plan 9, the same idea as the QEMU VNC
    route we just set up.
  - drawterm only shows up inside the description of CINAP_LENREK's
    devfs-win32.c. yacc only shows up as APM's patch for building the Go parser.

  Nothing in the index matches your core work. I searched for lalr, wirth,
  oberon, euclid, verilog, braille and domino, and none of them appear. The
  index does have the general patterns your ports use: 9P file servers,
  browsers, and ways of moving files between machines. It has nothing for the
  LALR reading of Wirth's notation, the Elements, the Braille and Aegean casts,
  or the dominoes.

  I kept the relay's own framing as its reading, not yours. That covers its
  claim that your work "stands above" the index and "goes on the map next", and
  its comparisons (divergefs as like cube256.ts, hubfs as like the portal).

● I read the page and compared it with our guest. I recorded it as the cue
  standalone-cpu in global.vtt (e7ca8ef).

  The guest is already most of the way there. helix is a CPU server listening on
  17010. What it's missing is its own auth server: as shipped, /lib/ndb/local
  points authentication at Bell Labs' old server, which no longer answers.
  That's why drawterm can't log in, as the PLAN9 README already noted. The
  page's steps that would fix it are:

  - an authdom= / auth=helix entry in ndb,
  - resetting nvram so the next boot asks for the bootes key,
  - keyfs and changeuser bootes,
  - the auth listener on port 567.

  I didn't apply them, since you said we don't need auth. The screen stays on
  VNC.

  One part of the page does help the VNC route. Its optional startup lines for a
  CPU server are:

  aux/mouse $mouseport
  aux/vga -l $vgasize
  exec rio

  I ran the aux/vga step and rio by hand earlier. aux/mouse is the step I
  haven't taken. It's probably what the pointer needs to work through VNC. If
  you want, I can reboot the guest, add those three lines to its startup so it
  comes up straight into rio with a working mouse, and check it in a viewer.

● This is the fortune file from the Bell Labs Unix and Plan 9 line, the one the
  fortune program draws from. Your paste was cut off by the 50,000-character
  limit at "Let's just take this shortcut across this field…", so I only have it
  up to there. The guest's screen is running rio right now, so I couldn't check
  this against the copy inside Plan 9. I recorded it as the cue fortunes in
  global.vtt (1c56d0a).

  Some lines in it touch the record directly:

  - "Atomic compare and exchange. These sequences are not actually atomic … very
    close to atomic": the protocol's primitive.
  - "Brashith Alhim": the first two words of Genesis 1:1, bereshit elohim, in an
    old transliteration. ELOHIM has its own codex.
  - "Inside every large problem is a small problem struggling to get out." –N.
    Wirth
  - "God made the integers; all else is the work of Man."
  - "Let no one unskilled in geometry enter here." –Plato: the Elements.
  - "355/113 — not the famous irrational number PI, but an incredible
    simulation." I checked this one. 355/113 is the fourth best approximation
    from pi's continued fraction:

  | Approximation | Off from pi by |
  |---------------|----------------|
  | 3             | 0.14           |
  | 22/7          | 0.0013         |
  | 333/106       | 0.00008        |
  | 355/113       | 0.0000003      |
    It's that close because the next term in pi's continued fraction is
    unusually large (292). It's the same continued-fraction mechanism as Euclid
    I.3 in I-256.
  - "A big book is a big nuisance." –Callimachus, librarian of Alexandria

❯ lets do a lexical analysis oof the braille aegean and dominoes distribution

  variations as the comparison. For each, I'll count words and their lengths,
  the 6-dot and 8-dot Braille cells, the nibble dominoes and their parity, and
  the digits cast into the five Aegean sets.

  ⎿  (No output)
● I wrote scripts/lexical-casts.mjs (../boxedPINE/scripts/lexical-casts.mjs) and
  ran it on three texts: the Plan 9 fortunes, the three Elements statements,
  and the 15 variations. Its usage is node --experimental-strip-types
  scripts/lexical-casts.mjs FILE …. The script is committed (60c7170) and the
  results are recorded as the cue lexical-casts (25613f9). I didn't commit the
  fortunes text itself.

  Words

  ┌────────────┬────────┬──────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │            │ Words  │ Distinct │ Short enough to be tags (1–8 letters) │
  ├────────────┼────────┼──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Fortunes   │ 48,323 │ 9,549    │ 93.7%                                 │
  ├────────────┼────────┼──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Elements   │ 47     │ 26       │ 93.6%                                 │
  ├────────────┼────────┼──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Variations │ 27     │ 14       │ 100%                                  │
  └────────────┴────────┴──────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  Braille, 6-dot cells

  ┌────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │            │  Cells used  │ Dot places empty │
  ├────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Fortunes   │ all 64 of 64 │ 59.6%            │
  ├────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Elements   │ 26 of 64     │ 59.0%            │
  ├────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Variations │ 17 of 64     │ 54.4%            │
  └────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────┘

  So in running English text, about 59–60% of the dot places stay empty, whether
  it's a joke file or Euclid. The variations, written in capitals, leave fewer
  empty.

  Dominoes

  Each byte is a domino of two nibbles, and the 8-dot Braille cell is the same
  byte, so they share one count.

  - Fortunes: use 146 of the 256 possible dominoes, and 3.3% are doubles (both
    halves equal).
  - Elements: 1.9% doubles.
  - Variations: 12.4% doubles, almost all from the letter D (0x44, a 4|4 double)
    in GOD, WORD, ADAM and ADONAI.

  I also took each fortune line's parity: all its nibbles XORed together, which
  is the gate's nibble. The 16 values are slightly uneven. A chi-square test
  gives p ≈ 0.03, so the unevenness is unlikely to be pure chance, though it's
  not strong. The two most common values are 6 and 9, which are each other's
  complements (6 XOR F = 9). I noted that only as a count.

  Aegean

  - The fortunes contain 1,350 runs of digits, and casting them through the five
    sets uses all 45 Aegean signs.
  - By position, each set gets about half as many digits as the one before
    (1257, 627, 299, 178, 91), because most numbers are short.
  - The first digits roughly follow Benford's law at 1 and 2 (28.6% against an
    expected 30.1%; 17.8% against 17.6%). They run high at 8 and 9 (7.7% and
    7.5%, against 5.1% and 4.6%).
  - The Elements and the variations contain no digits, so they have no Aegean
    cast.

           +sign alone (U+10107 to U+10133, nine a set). Its "Babel tower
           +" is the page's word for XML; the author's tower of Babel (en

           +pt as the author's reading, unstated: the page beside the cas
           +ts.                                                          

● This is cat-v's page against standards. It's mostly quotations, and it names
  one good encoding (UTF-8) and a list of bad standards. I recorded it as the
  cue harmful-standards in global.vtt (23f1d5e).

  Three points line up with the work:

  - UTF-8. The page calls it "the only sane character encoding, invented by Ken
    Thompson." Every cast we built reads its bytes as UTF-8: the dominoes room,
    the domino cube and the lexical analysis.
  - HTML is on its bad list. That puts all the pages in space/ on the wrong side
    of the page's view. That's the page's judgement, not a finding.
  - Doug's passage on notation. He says Roman numerals hobbled mathematics, and
    that bad notation "can't be read right to left or left to right." The Aegean
    numbers avoid this by design. Each decimal place has its own set of nine
    signs, so a number's value is just the sum of its signs, the same whichever
    direction you read it, and no zero sign is needed. I checked that each sign
    alone tells you its set. This is the property the five-set cycle in the
    dominoes relies on.

  The page also calls XML "the XML Babel tower." That's its own use of the
  image. Your tower of Babel reading of YHWH is a separate use, and I kept them
  apart.

❯ yes we use braille aegean and domenoes to normalize all binary sequences to
  utf8 and a specific block design

      1092 +2026-11-04 the author, of harmful-standards: "yes we use brai
           +lle aegean and domenoes to normalize all binary sequences to 
           +utf8 and a specific block design". Checked, the UTF-8: every 
           +byte b is the 8-dot Braille cell U+2800 + b, and all 256 enco
           +de in UTF-8 as three bytes, E2 A0 80 (b = 0) to E2 A3 BF (b =
           + 255): any binary sequence is thus a valid UTF-8 text, one ce
           +ll a byte, and back; an Aegean sign is four bytes (ONE, U+101
           +07, is F0 90 84 87); a domino is the byte's two nibbles. Chec
           +ked, a block design inside the 256 cells, dots 1 to 8 as eigh
           +t points: the extended Hamming code [8, 4, 4] (generated by 1
           +1110000, 11001100, 10101010, 11111111) has 16 codewords, by w
           +eight one of 0, fourteen of 4, one of 8; the fourteen 4-dot c
           +ells are the blocks of the 3-(8, 4, 1) design, every one of t
           +he 56 triples of dots in exactly one block (computed), the de
           +sign of two tetrahedra in The Tetrahedral Superposition (bloc
           +k-design-mechanism); the seven blocks through dot 8, with dot
           + 8 dropped, are the Fano plane, 2-(7, 3, 1) (computed). Its c
           +ells: ⠀⣰⣌⠼⢪⡚⡦⢖⣿⠏⠳⣃⡕⢥⢙⡩. Found, a count only: 16 codewords as 
           +1 + 14 + 1, the empty cell, fourteen blocks and the full cell
           +, as each character's set of 16 is fourteen Fano planes and t

● Your normalization claim holds up, and it lands on a block design that is
  already in the record. I recorded it as the cue utf8-block-design (d243b40).

  UTF-8. Every byte b is the 8-dot Braille cell U+2800 + b, and all 256 of those
  cells are three bytes in UTF-8. So any binary sequence becomes valid UTF-8
  text, one cell per byte, and converts back exactly. An Aegean sign takes four
  bytes in UTF-8. A domino is a byte's two nibbles.

  The block design. I treated the eight Braille dots as eight points and used
  the extended Hamming code, a standard error-correcting code on 8 bits. It has
  16 codewords:

  ┌──────────────────────┬───────┐
  │     Dots filled      │ Count │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ 0 (the empty cell ⠀) │ 1     │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ 4                    │ 14    │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ 8 (the full cell ⣿)  │ 1     │
  └──────────────────────┴───────┘

  The 14 four-dot cells form the 3-(8,4,1) design from your Tetrahedral
  Superposition note. I checked all 56 possible sets of three dots, and each
  lies in exactly one of the 14 cells. The seven cells that include dot 8, with
  dot 8 removed, form the Fano plane. The 16 cells are:

  ⠀⣰⣌⠼⢪⡚⡦⢖⣿⠏⠳⣃⡕⢥⢙⡩

  The split 1 + 14 + 1 matches each character's set of 16 in the Commons:
  fourteen Fano planes, plus the two Hasse diagrams at places 0 and 15. I
  recorded that match as a count only.

  How far the code normalizes a byte:

  ┌───────┬──────────────────────────┬────────────────────┐
  │ Bytes │ Distance from a codeword │   Normalizes to    │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 16    │ are codewords            │ themselves         │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 128   │ one dot from exactly one │ that one codeword  │
  ├───────┼──────────────────────────┼────────────────────┤
  │ 112   │ two dots from several    │ no single codeword │
  └───────┴──────────────────────────┴────────────────────┘

  So 144 of the 256 bytes normalize to the code with no choice involved. The
  other 112 would need a rule to break ties.

  harmful.cat-v.org. It's the site's "Encyclopedia of things considered
  harmful," with sections on software, standards, economics, society and others.
  I recorded it as harmful-index (4ddf253). Its opinions are the site's, not
  the record's. So far I've only read its standards page.

● HTTP 0.2 is cat-v's proposal for "a minimalist and sane subset of HTTP that
  maps well to 9P." It allows GET and POST only, uses UTF-8 everywhere, and has
  a simplified grammar. It drops cookies, query strings and pipelining, and it
  leaves "How to handle auth?" open. I checked our two HTTP servers against it
  and recorded the result as the cue http02 (24c1429).

  ┌──────────┬──────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │          │   Agent bridge (boxedPINE)   │    World server in Plan 9     │
  │          │                              │            (mkd.c)            │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │          │ GET and POST only, plus      │ GET for tiles, events and     │
  │ Methods  │ OPTIONS for the browser's    │ state; POST for events ("Only │
  │          │ cross-origin check           │  POST writes")                │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Cookies  │ none                         │ none                          │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ Query    │ /agent/inbox?since=          │ /events?SPACE&Z/X/Y           │
  │ strings  │                              │                               │
  └──────────┴──────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  So both already follow HTTP 0.2's two-method rule and use no cookies. The
  bridge names agents by tag in the Host or the path, which works like a 9P file
  name.

  Query strings are where we break from it. The world server's ?SPACE&Z/X/Y, the
  bridge's ?since=, and the portal's ?agents=, ?world= and ?vtt= are all things
  HTTP 0.2 wants removed.

  Two more points line up with the proposal. Its "simplified BNF grammar" is
  what the record already does with Wirth's notation and the LALR build. Its
  open auth question matches your "we don't need the auth."

● I checked the repetend as a linear system, and it holds. Recorded as the cue
  path-is-query (960d8c4).

  A repeating expansion x = 0.(D), with a block D of L digits repeating,
  satisfies one linear equation: bᴸ·x − x = D, where b is the base. Solving it
  gives back the fraction:

  - 1/17: (10¹⁶ − 1)·x = 0588235294117647 gives x = 1/17. I computed it.
  - 1/6 = 0.1666…: with a digit before the repeating part, you need two
    equations in the same x. 100x = 16.666… and 10x = 1.666…. Subtracting gives
    90x = 15, so x = 1/6.

  So the digits, read as a path, pin down the value with nothing else needed.

  In the servers, the query strings would turn into path segments:

  - the world server's /events?SPACE&Z/X/Y would become /events/SPACE/Z/X/Y
  - the bridge's ?since= would become one more segment

  I haven't changed either server. Say if you want them converted.

❯ Very often, and in this article, the coefficients and solutions of the
  equations are constrained to be real or complex numbers, but the theory and
  algorithms apply to coefficients and solutions in any field. For other
  algebraic structures, other theories have been developed. For coefficients
  and solutions in an integral domain, such as the ring of integers, see Linear
  equation over a ring. For coefficients and solutions that are polynomials,
  see Gröbner basis. For finding the "best" integer solutions among many, see
  Integer linear programming. For an example of a more exotic structure to
  which linear algebra can be applied, see Tropical geometry.

❯ One extremely helpful view is that each unknown is a weight for a column
  vector in a linear combination.
  x
  1
  [
  a
  11
  a
  21
  ⋮
  a
  m
  1
  ]
  +
  x
  2
  [
  a
  12
  a
  22
  ⋮
  a
  m
  2
  ]
  +
  ⋯
  +
  x
  n
  [
  a
  1
  n
  a
  2
  n
  ⋮
  a
  m
  n
  ]
  =
  [
  b
  1
  b
  2
  ⋮
  b
  m
  ]
  {\displaystyle x_{1}{\begin{bmatrix}a_{11}\\a_{21}\\\vdots
  \\a_{m1}\end{bmatrix}}+x_{2}{\begin{bmatrix}a_{12}\\a_{22}\\\vdots
  \\a_{m2}\end{bmatrix}}+\dots +x_{n}{\begin{bmatrix}a_{1n}\\a_{2n}\\\vdots
  \\a_{mn}\end{bmatrix}}={\begin{bmatrix}b_{1}\\b_{2}\\\vdots
  \\b_{m}\end{bmatrix}}}This allows all the language and theory of vector
  spaces (or more generally, modules) to be brought to bear. For example, the
  collection of all possible linear combinations of the vectors on the
  left-hand side (LHS) is called their span, and the equations have a solution
  just when the right-hand vector is within that span. If every vector within
  that span has exactly one expression as a linear combination of the given
  left-hand vectors, then any solution is unique. In any event, the span has a
  basis of linearly independent vectors that do guarantee exactly one
  expression; and the number of vectors in that basis (its dimension) cannot be
  larger than m or n, but it can be smaller. This is important because if we
  have m independent vectors a solution is guaranteed regardless of the
  right-hand side (RHS), and otherwise not guaranteed.

❯ Solution set

  The solution set for the equations x − y = −1 and 3x + y = 9 is the single
  point (2, 3).
  A solution of a linear system is an assignment of values to the variables
  x
  1
  ,
  x
  2
  ,
  …
  ,
  x
  n
  {\displaystyle x_{1},x_{2},\dots ,x_{n}} such that each of the equations is
  satisfied. The set of all possible solutions is called the solution set.[5]

  A linear system may behave in any one of three possible ways:

  The system has infinitely many solutions.
  The system has a unique solution.
  The system has no solution.
  Geometric interpretation
  For a system involving two variables (x and y), each linear equation
  determines a line on the xy-plane. Because a solution to a linear system must
  satisfy all of the equations, the solution set is the intersection of these
  lines, and is hence either a line, a single point, or the empty set.

  For three variables, each linear equation determines a plane in
  three-dimensional space, and the solution set is the intersection of these
  planes. Thus the solution set may be a plane, a line, a single point, or the
  empty set. For example, as three parallel planes do not have a common point,
  the solution set of their equations is empty; the solution set of the
  equations of three planes intersecting at a point is single point; if three
  planes pass through two points, their equations have at least two common
  solutions; in fact the solution set is infinite and consists in all the line
  passing through these points.[6]

  For n variables, each linear equation determines a hyperplane in
  n-dimensional space. The solution set is the intersection of these
  hyperplanes, and is a flat, which may have any dimension lower than n.

  General behavior

  The solution set for two equations in three variables is, in general, a line.
  In general, the behavior of a linear system is determined by the relationship
  between the number of equations and the number of unknowns. Here, "in
  general" means that a different behavior may occur for specific values of the
  coefficients of the equations.

  In general, a system with fewer equations than unknowns has infinitely many
  solutions, but it may have no solution. Such a system is known as an
  underdetermined system.
  In general, a system with the same number of equations and unknowns has a
  single unique solution.
  In general, a system with more equations than unknowns has no solution. Such
  a system is also known as an overdetermined system.
  In the first case, the dimension of the solution set is, in general, equal to
  n − m, where n is the number of variables and m is the number of equations.

  The following pictures illustrate this trichotomy in the case of two
  variables:

  One equation    Two equations   Three equations
  The first system has infinitely many solutions, namely all of the points on
  the blue line. The second system has a single unique solution, namely the
  intersection of the two lines. The third system has no solutions, since the
  three lines share no common point.

  It must be kept in mind that the pictures above show only the most common
  case (the general case). It is possible for a system of two equations and two
  unknowns to have no solution (if the two lines are parallel), or for a
  system of three equations and two unknowns to be solvable (if the three lines
  intersect at a single point).

  A system of linear equations behave differently from the general case if the
  equations are linearly dependent, or if it is inconsistent and has no more
  equations than unknowns.

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

  Linear systems (cue linear-systems): the field theory applies directly to the
  block-design code from before.
  - Over the two-element field, the four generator bytes are four independent
    columns. Their span is exactly the 16 codewords.
  - So among the 256 bytes, exactly 16 have a solution, and each has only one.
  - The other 240 have none. Normalizing one means moving it to the nearest
    solvable byte.
  - The repetend equations are the square case: one unknown and one equation,
    with a single solution. I also checked the article's example: x − y = −1 and
    3x + y = 9 meet at (2, 3).

● Bash(echo '- [Numbers as index pairs](numbers-as-index-pairs.md) — the author

● I'll read numbers in pairs with you from now on. I saved that to memory so it
  carries into later sessions.

  The page's numbers, in pairs
  - The site says it has the stories of "over 670,000 people" harmed by someone
    not thinking critically. That's 67 00 00.
  - Its counter shows 368,379 killed (36 83 79) and 306,096 injured (30 60 96).
    Those add to 674,475, which is 67 44 75.
  - The money figure, $2,815,931,000, is 28 15 93 10 00.

  6/7, read in pairs. This gives a clean result. A turn by one pair multiplies
  by 100, and 100 leaves 2 when divided by 7. So in pairs, the six fractions k/7
  fall into exactly two cycles:

  ┌───────┬───────────────┬────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Cycle │   Fractions   │             Pairs              │
  ├───────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ A     │ 1/7, 2/7, 4/7 │ 14 28 57 → 28 57 14 → 57 14 28 │
  ├───────┼───────────────┼────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B     │ 3/7, 6/7, 5/7 │ 42 85 71 → 85 71 42 → 71 42 85 │
  └───────┴───────────────┴────────────────────────────────┘

  - Cycle A's set {1, 2, 4} is the Fano plane's difference set. Its differences
    mod 7 give each of 1 to 6 exactly once (I checked). Its seven shifts are the
    seven lines of the Fano plane. Cycle B holds the other three remainders.
  - 1/7 and 6/7 sit in opposite cycles, and they cancel pair by pair. 14 + 85 =
    28 + 71 = 57 + 42 = 99, so 1/7 + 6/7 = 0.999… = 1. That fits your
    "cancelling out" if the two cycles are read as the two halves.
  - The pairs build up by doubling. 1/7 = 14/98, so 1/7 is the pairs 14, 28, 56,
    112 … overlapping with carries. 6/7 = 84/98 the same way.
  - The 67 in 67 00 00: 1/67 repeats over 33 digits, and in base 100 it repeats
    over 33 pairs.

  I recorded this as the cue index-pairs (460fa1b). The logic tree for
  cancelling harmful logic, and 67 00 00 as a degree of the Lie algebra, are
  kept as your reading.

❯ see these people get it and they say this stuff in snide jokes because the
  proof is apparent but no one looks
  List:       9fans
  Subject:    Re: [9fans] v8 shell
  From:       "rob pike, esq." <r () geekmail ! cc>
  Date:       2003-11-13 17:41:24
  Message-ID: e004e732ced8e1653a716c793de55e43 () google ! com
  [Download RAW message or body]

  i fought fairly hard to get the key features of the v8 shell adopted
  into posix.  these included exportable functions and output from
  commands like whatis that could be read as input.  but i didn't fight
  hard enough because i didn't fight at every single committee meeting.
  as soon as i stopped going, the changes were reverted ("thank god he's
  gone").  the experience soured me for life on contributing to
  standards committees.

  for example, the 'builtin' trick in modern shells comes out of the v8
  shell but the other half is missing.

  korn shell or bash:

  $ type cd
  cd is a shell builtin
  $
  v8 shell:

  $ whatis cd
  builtin cd
  $
  see the difference? the latter is executable code.  why was it important
  to `fix' this in the committee?  bitterness rules.

  maybe that's why rc happened.  probably was a factor.  i know i asked
  td to design rc, but i also did the work in creating the v8 shell. i
  don't really remember.

  -rob

● Pike's point holds in practice, so I ran it in our Plan 9 guest.

  helix# fn greet {echo hello from rc}; whatis cd greet path
  builtin cd
  fn greet {echo hello from rc}
  path=(. /bin)

  Every line whatis prints is runnable rc code. I saved whatis greet to a file,
  redefined greet to something else, then read the file back with .. Running
  greet again printed hello from rc, so the original was restored. The
  description really is the program, as he says.

  On this machine's bash, type cd gives "cd is a shell builtin" and type greet
  gives "greet is a function". Both are just labels. bash's declare -f greet
  does print re-readable code, so the missing half Pike points to is
  specifically in type, the command he compared against.

  The record relies on the same property:

  - each cue's text is the record itself;
  - the twin test replays from a literal trace;
  - the WSN grammars are read by the same parser that checks them.

  In each case the description can be executed. I recorded it as the cue whatis
  (5ca77cd), with your reading that the joke is the proof in plain sight.

● There's nothing to be embarrassed about. Following words back to their literal
  meanings, and finding that it led you somewhere, is your own account, and I
  recorded it in your words as the cue anchors (beaba75).

  The cat-v page argues that a word does harm through lack of meaning, not
  through its meaning, because then anyone can attach whatever they find
  convenient. By that test, your four anchors aren't harmful here, because you
  already defined each one in writing in axiomatic-sovereignty
  (../../omi/axiomatic-sovereignty/_dev-docs/Freedom,%20Autonomy,%20Sovereignty,
  %20and%20Reciprocity.md):

  - Freedom: "the capacity to receive, consider, reject, preserve, modify, or
    propagate knowledge without imposed domination."
  - Autonomy: "the capacity of a conscious being or community to reason, choose,
    and act without being absorbed into an authority they did not freely
    accept."
  - Sovereignty: "the responsibility to govern one's own participation, conduct,
    dependencies, and consequences."
  - Reciprocity: "the recognition that sovereign capacity is often made possible
    by what others have preserved, discovered, verified, taught, corrected, or
    propagated." It "does not create a debt of obedience."

  Your idea of a governance or directive layer that carries no connotation
  beyond self-control fits that: it would apply only those four written
  definitions, and nothing else would get attached to it. I kept that as your
  reading.

● That's the purpose stated plainly, and the byte level already supports it. I
  took one word, GOD, through four of the codex witnesses and turned each into
  Braille byte cells, one cell per byte:

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬───────┬──────────────────┐
  │           Witness            │ Bytes │      Cells       │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────┼──────────────────┤
  │ GOD                          │ 3     │ ⡇⡏⡄              │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────┼──────────────────┤
  │ ΘΕΟΣ (Greek)                 │ 8     │ ⣎⢘⣎⢕⣎⢟⣎⢣         │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────┼──────────────────┤
  │ אֱלֹהִים (Hebrew)               │ 16    │ ⣗⢐⣖⢱⣗⢜⣖⢹⣗⢔⣖⢴⣗⢙⣗⢝ │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼───────┼──────────────────┤
  │ Auramazda 𐎠𐎢𐎼… (Old Persian) │ 28    │ ⣰⢐⢎⢠⣰⢐⢎⢢…        │
  └──────────────────────────────┴───────┴──────────────────┘

  All four now share one form, yet each still shows where it came from. Every
  character's first cell names its script:

  - Latin letters are a single cell each.
  - Greek letters start with ⣎.
  - Old Persian characters start with ⣰⢐.
  - Hebrew letters start with ⣗, but the vowel points start with ⣖. The pointing
    was added by the Masoretes centuries after the consonants, and that later
    layer can be told apart from the letters by its first cell alone. That's
    provenance carried in the bytes themselves.

  One count, recorded without reading anything into it: Old Persian's first cell

  (i) Never use a metaphor, simile, or other figure of speech which you are

  (iii) If it is possible to cut a word out, always cut it out.

  (iv) Never use the passive where you can use the active.

  (v) Never use a foreign phrase, a scientific word, or a jargon word if you
  can think of an everyday English equivalent.

  (vi) Break any of these rules sooner than say anything outright barbarous.

● I recorded it as the cue rule-four (845f045). I checked Orwell's six rules
  against the essay's page, and your paste matches it, including the typo "Never
  us" in rule (ii).

  Rule four is "Never use the passive where you can use the active." Read beside
  the dominoes:

  - What a passive sentence hides. An active sentence names who acts on what, in
    order. A passive one can leave the actor out entirely: "it was changed."
  - What a domino path can't hide. A path is ordered the same way as an active
    sentence: each joint sets the end of one domino against the start of the
    next. So a path can't leave its actor unnamed.
  - The strict modal. In I-231 your strict modal is Atomics.compareExchange. It
    names its cell, the value it expects and the value it writes. If the
    condition fails it changes nothing; if it holds it changes exactly one cell.
    That is a fully stated active statement, with nothing hidden in a passive.
    Your template note (ZIGUAROTH 06d866d) says the same: "a template is
    basically a strict modal operator, nothing changes but the strict logic
    overlay."

  The Elements statements fit rule four with one exception. Their verbs are
  active ("to construct," "to place," "to cut off"). But "given" appears four
  times, and that's a passive participle: each proposition's starting point is
  simply "given," by no one named. In the cue I first wrote five; I added a
  correction (f8bffa1).

  Your reading is kept as yours: the dominoes as a path that enforces the
  predicate logic of the strict modal, by rule four.

  not denotations, but given is the round-trip action between parties, like in
  the begat, beget or how a man knows a woman in a bblical connattion cmpared
  too, to know

● You're right, and I've taken back my reading of "given" as passive. I recorded
  your correction as the cue given-round-trip (00a9297).

  Atomics.compareExchange behaves exactly as you describe. It always hands the
  caller back the value it found, and it takes the new value only if the found
  value was the one expected. I ran it on a cell holding 5:

  ┌────────────────┬──────────┬─────────────────┐
  │      Call      │ Returned │ Cell afterwards │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ exchange 5 → 9 │ 5        │ 9 (accepted)    │
  ├────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ exchange 5 → 7 │ 9        │ 9 (refused)     │
  └────────────────┴──────────┴─────────────────┘

  Either way the caller is given the observed state, whether or not the exchange
  was accepted. So giving and accepting are two separate acts joined in one
  call, as you said: to give doesn't mean it was accepted, and to accept doesn't
  mean it was given.

  The cue also names your biblical comparisons: begat (Hebrew yalad, to beget)
  and to know (yada, as in Genesis 4:1, "Adam knew Eve his wife"). Each is an
  act between two parties whose result is a third thing, the given. Their source
  texts are in the codex's Leningrad and KJV witnesses; I haven't cast them
  yet.

● Here it is. I recorded it as the cue know-beget-give (35f0d3c). I ran it from
  your private witness copies and wrote nothing into the codex.

  In the KJV, by Strong's number:

  ┌───────────────────┬──────────────┬────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │      Hebrew       │ Times in the │  First at  │  Most common English   │
  │                   │      KJV     │            │                        │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ yada, to know     │ 941 (09 41)  │ Genesis    │ know 430, known 105,   │
  │ (H3045)           │              │ 3:5        │ knew 83                │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ yalad, to beget   │ 497 (04 97)  │ Genesis    │ begat 179, bare 111,   │
  │ (H3205)           │              │ 3:16       │ born 79                │
  ├───────────────────┼──────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ natan, to give    │ 2007 (20 07) │ Genesis    │ give 468, gave 251,    │
  │ (H5414)           │              │ 1:17       │ given 249              │
  └───────────────────┴──────────────┴────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  Genesis 4:1 holds your round trip in one verse. "Adam knew [yada] Eve his
  wife; and she conceived, and bare [yalad] Cain, and said, I have gotten a man
  from the LORD." The KJV's own margin note reads: "Cain: that is, Gotten, or,
  Acquired." So the verse has knowing, then bearing, then a third party named
  for what was gotten.

  Genesis 5:3 has "begat a son in his own likeness." The Leningrad word there is
  ויולד (vayyoled).

  The codex casts. These are the same functions the variations use: the word's
  bytes as Braille cells, then XORed against YHWH.

  ┌─────────────┬────────────┬──────────┐
  │    Word     │ Byte cells │ XOR YHWH │
  ├─────────────┼────────────┼──────────┤
  │ ידע (yada)  │ ⣗⢙⣗⢓⣗⢢     │ ⢎⣑⢀⣛⣗⢢   │
  ├─────────────┼────────────┼──────────┤
  │ ילד (yalad) │ ⣗⢙⣗⢜⣗⢓     │ ⢎⣑⢀⣔⣗⢓   │
  ├─────────────┼────────────┼──────────┤
  │ נתן (natan) │ ⣗⢠⣗⢪⣗⢟     │ ⢎⣨⢀⣢⣗⢟   │
  ├─────────────┼────────────┼──────────┤
  │ KNEW        │ ⡋⡎⡅⡗       │ ⠒⠆⠒⠟     │
  ├─────────────┼────────────┼──────────┤
  │ BEGAT       │ ⡂⡅⡇⡁⡔      │ ⠛⠍⠐⠉⡔    │
  ├─────────────┼────────────┼──────────┤
  │ GIVEN       │ ⡇⡉⡖⡅⡎      │ ⠞⠁⠁⠍⡎    │
  └─────────────┴────────────┴──────────┘

  One thing stands out. Yada and yalad both begin with yod (י), the first letter
  of the Name. So their XOR against YHWH matches in the first three cells, ⢎⣑⢀,
  and only differs after that. Natan begins with nun (נ), and it splits from
  them at the second cell (⣨ instead of ⣑).

● It relates exactly, at the level of the letters. I checked it against
  Leningrad, Genesis 2:23: "she shall be called Woman (ishah), because she was
  taken out of Man (ish)."

  Man, woman, fire, YAH:

  ┌────────────────────┬────────────────────┬────────┬───────────────┐
  │        Word        │      Letters       │ Remove │    Leaves     │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────┼───────────────┤
  │ ish, man (איש)     │ aleph · yod · shin │ yod    │ אש, esh, fire │
  ├────────────────────┼────────────────────┼────────┼───────────────┤
  │ ishah, woman (אשה) │ aleph · shin · he  │ he     │ אש, esh, fire │
  └────────────────────┴────────────────────┴────────┴───────────────┘

  The two letters removed, yod and he, together spell יה, YAH. So man and woman
  without YAH are both fire.

  The letter values agree: man 311 − 10 (yod) = woman 306 − 5 (he) = fire 301,
  and YAH = 10 + 5 = 15. This is the traditional teaching from the Talmud, Sotah
  17a: if man and woman are worthy, the divine Presence is between them; if
  not, fire consumes them. I'm citing that from memory, not from a text here.

  The earth: adamah, ground (אדמה), minus its he gives אדמ. That becomes adam
  (אדם) once its last letter takes the final form ם. Final mem sits one code
  point below ordinary mem, which is the final-form rule recorded in I-237. The
  values match too: adam 45, adamah 50, and the he is 5 again.

  In the casts: yod and he begin with the same Braille cell (⣗) and differ only
  in their second byte:

  ┌───────┬────────────┐
  │ Word  │ Byte cells │
  ├───────┼────────────┤
  │ ish   │ ⣗⢐⣗⢙⣗⢩     │
  ├───────┼────────────┤
  │ ishah │ ⣗⢐⣗⢩⣗⢔     │
  ├───────┼────────────┤
  │ esh   │ ⣗⢐⣗⢩       │
  ├───────┼────────────┤
  │ YAH   │ ⣗⢙⣗⢔       │
  └───────┴────────────┘

  All six terms (YAH, YOD, ADAM, EVE, EARTH, FIRE) are already among your
  variations. Recorded as the cue fire-without-yah (a3940ca).

❯ also the yada, yalad nd natan, share the vowel pattern but consta differen is
  like the god is|of word varaitns
  Word    Byte cells      XOR YHWH
  ידע (yada)      ⣗⢙⣗⢓⣗⢢  ⢎⣑⢀⣛⣗⢢
  ילד (yalad)     ⣗⢙⣗⢜⣗⢓  ⢎⣑⢀⣔⣗⢓
  נתן (natan)     ⣗⢠⣗⢪⣗⢟  ⢎⣨⢀⣢⣗⢟

● Yes, and the bytes show it exactly. I used the pointed dictionary forms, which
  all share the same vowels: qamets after the first consonant and patah after
  the second.

  ┌─────────────┬────────────────┐
  │    Word     │   Byte cells   │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ יָדַע (yada)  │ ⣗⢙ ⣖⢸ ⣗⢓ ⣖⢷ ⣗⢢ │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ יָלַד (yalad) │ ⣗⢙ ⣖⢸ ⣗⢜ ⣖⢷ ⣗⢓ │
  ├─────────────┼────────────────┤
  │ נָתַן (natan) │ ⣗⢠ ⣖⢸ ⣗⢪ ⣖⢷ ⣗⢟ │
  └─────────────┴────────────────┘

  XORing them byte by byte, the vowel frame cancels to empty cells (⠀). Only the
  consonants that differ leave a mark:

  yada  ⊕ yalad   ⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀⠏⠀⠀⠀⠱    (the shared yod cancels too)
  yada  ⊕ natan   ⠀⠹⠀⠀⠀⠹⠀⠀⠀⠽

  The variations behave the same way:

  GOD IS WORD ⊕ GOD OF WORD    ⠀⠀⠀⠀⠆⠕⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀    (the frame GOD _ WORD cancels;
  only the middle word is left)
  GOD WAS WORD ⊕ WORD WAS GOD  ⠐⠀⠖⡤⡷⠖⠒⡳⡷⠈⠝⠀    (order changed: almost nothing
  cancels)

  So the vowel pattern is the verbs' frame in the same way GOD _ WORD is the
  variations' frame. A shared frame shows up as zeros, and a changed slot is the
  only mark left.

  One count I noted without reading into it: yada ⊕ natan gives the same cell ⠹
  at both of its first two consonants. That happens because yod ⊕ nun and dalet
  ⊕ tav both equal 0x39.

  I recorded this as the cue vowel-frame (a3691f3), with your comparison marked
  as holding in the bytes.

● I bridged them across three witnesses: Leningrad (Hebrew), Swete (Greek) and
  the KJV with Strong's numbers. I recorded it as the cue lord-image-bridge
  (1bda7c1). Nothing was written into the codex.

  Genesis 15:2, the first time Adonai appears:

  ┌───────────┬───────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
  │ Leningrad │     Swete     │           KJV            │
  ├───────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────┤
  │ אדני יהוה │ Δέσποτα κύριε │ Lord [H0136] GOD [H3069] │
  └───────────┴───────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  The two words cross over. Adonai becomes Δέσποτα ("master") and "Lord". The
  Name becomes κύριε and "GOD" in small capitals. So in this verse κύριος
  translates the Name, not Adonai.

  Counts, in your pairs:

  - In the KJV, Adonai (H0136) appears 434 times (04 34), almost always as
    "Lord".
  - The Name (H3068) appears 6,518 times (65 18), as "LORD".
  - The Name written with Elohim's vowels (H3069) appears 305 times (03 05), as
    "GOD".
  - Swete's Genesis has κύριος and θεός 226 times each.

  Genesis 1:27, imago dei:

  ┌────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
  │ Leningrad  │      Swete       │         KJV         │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────┤
  │ בצלם אלהים │ κατ’ εἰκόνα θεοῦ │ in the image of God │
  └────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────┘

  The Latin imago Dei (the Vulgate's "ad imaginem Dei") isn't among your
  sources, so I have no witness for it.

  The frames cancel, as with the verbs. XORing related forms leaves empty cells
  wherever they share a frame:

  THEOS ⊕ LOGOS              ⠘⠇⠂⠀⠀           the -OS cancels (I-215's family)
  IMAGO DEI ⊕ IMAGE OF GOD   ⠀⠀⠀⠀⠊⠀⠋⠃⡩      IMAG and the space cancel
  ΘΕΟΣ ⊕ ΘΕΟΥ                ⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀⠀⠆      "God" vs "of God": only the case
  ending's last byte differs

  One more count: THEOS XOR YHWH gives ⠍⠀⠒⠇⡓. The empty second cell is there
  because THEOS has an H in the same place as YHWH's first H.

  - The compass: on 2026-11-03 you said "YAH is the first compas x,y,z," and we
    placed YAH as one cell of the 256-cube, at (89, 65, 72).

  So I checked which letters of the Name (yod, he, vav) appear in each word we
  cast today:

  ┌─────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │      Word       │ Letters of the Name it holds │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ ish (man)       │ yod                          │
  ├─────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ ishah (woman)   │ he                           │

● Transforming at will holds. Diffusing doesn't yet, in the strict sense. I
  tested both on a real binary file, ASABIYAH.gltf (5,638 bytes), and recorded
  the result as the cue transform-at-will (e29e1dc).

  Transform: every step can be undone.
  - As Braille cells, the file becomes 16,914 bytes of valid UTF-8 and converts
    back byte for byte.
  - XORed with YHWH (repeating) and then XORed again, it comes back identical.
  - The delta map (rotl1 ⊕ rotl3 ⊕ rotr2 ⊕ C) is a one-to-one map on the 256
    bytes, so it can be reversed too.

  A negative is caught. I flipped one bit at byte 100, and the file's nibble
  parity changed from 9 to 8. Two flips in the same nibble position would cancel
  out and slip past the parity, though.

  Diffuse: not yet. Every step here stays inside its own byte:
  - XOR changes one bit for each bit it touches.
  - The delta map spreads one bit to three bits, but all within the same byte.
  - Nothing carries a change from one byte into the next.

  To make a single change spread across the whole data, you'd need a step that
  mixes bytes together: a carry, a rotation across the whole buffer, or
  something like the compare-exchange's frame. That step doesn't exist yet. Say
  when you want it built.

  I read "hars" as harms, the 670,000 from the page, and kept your reading that
  they work as negatives (must-reject cases) to the golden ones.

      1172 +A limit on time-mixes, mine: the frames reach every byte, whi
           +ch is coverage; a change made in one byte reaches another onl
           +y if some frame's write depends on a byte it does not write (
           +the exchange's compare reads only its own cell, so today a ch
           +ange at one place still stays at that place). Coverage over t
           +ime is in place; carrying one byte's change into the others n
           +eeds the frame's write to read a neighbour, not yet so.      

● Time does cross the bytes, with one limit.

  Cues over time cover every byte. The exchange is Atomics.compareExchange(svg,
  60 ⊕ frame, 127, 63), so the frame sets which byte it touches. Over frames 0
  to 127, 60 ⊕ frame visits all 128 byte positions exactly once (I computed it).
  No single step mixes bytes, but the sequence of cues covers them all, one
  byte per frame. A cue's time is its frame.

  Each literal has its logical counterparts. Every codex literal has an XOR line
  and an XNOR line against a key. Those two are complements: byte by byte they
  add up to FF (checked on YHWH). So the literal, its XOR and its XNOR are one
  value read three ways.

  The limit. Reaching every byte isn't yet the same as spreading a change. The
  exchange's comparison reads only its own cell, so a change in one byte still
  stays in that byte. For a change to carry from one byte into the next, a
  frame's write would have to depend on a neighbouring byte. That's the one
  piece still missing.

  I recorded the finding as time-mixes (39f7b9f) and the limit as
  time-mixes-limit (99d6cf0).
